**Russia**,[^cite_note-13] or the **Russian Federation**,[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-16] is a country in [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) and [North Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Asia). It is the [largest country in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area), spanning [eleven time zones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_Russia) and sharing [land borders with fourteen countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borders_of_Russia).[^cite_note-18] With a population of over 140 million, Russia is the [most populous country in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_population) and the [ninth-most populous in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_population). It is a [highly urbanised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urbanization_by_sovereign_state) country, with three-fourths of its population living in urban areas. [Moscow](19004.md), the [most populous metropolitan area in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_metropolitan_areas_in_Europe), is the capital and [largest city of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Russia_by_population), while [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md) is its second-largest city and a [major cultural centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_and_culture_in_Saint_Petersburg).&#x20;

Human settlement on modern Russian territory dates back to the [Lower Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Paleolithic). The emergence of the [East Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) as a prominent group in Europe between the 3rd and 8th centuries AD led to the establishment of [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27) in the 9th century, which adopted [Orthodox Christianity](10186.md) from the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) in 988. Following the [disintegration of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disintegration_of_Kievan_Rus%27) in the 12th century, the [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow) became the dominant [Russian principality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_principality) and led the [unification of Russian lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gathering_of_the_Russian_lands) during the [late medieval period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_medieval_period), culminating in the formation of the [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia) in 1547. By the early 18th century, Russia had expanded vastly through [conquest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_conquest_of_Siberia), [annexation](18950961.md), and the [efforts of Russian explorers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_explorers). It was proclaimed as the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) in 1721, which became the [third-largest empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires) in history. The [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) of 1917 led to [the abolition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Revolution) of the [Russian monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_monarchy) and the creation of the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_SFSR), the first [socialist state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_state). Following the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), Russia became the [largest and principal constituent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union) of the newly established [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) in 1922. Amidst [rapid industrialisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization_in_the_Soviet_Union) in the 1930s, [millions died](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excess_mortality_in_the_Soviet_Union_under_Joseph_Stalin) under [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin). The Soviet Union played [a decisive role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_in_World_War_II) for the [Allies in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II) by leading large-scale efforts on the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)). During the [Cold War](325329.md), it emerged as a [superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superpower) and competed with the [United States](3434750.md) for [geopolitical dominance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geopolitical_dominance). The Soviet era saw some of the most significant [Russian technological achievements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Russian_innovation), including the [first human-made satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_1) and the [first human expedition into outer space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostok_1).&#x20;

In 1991, the Russian SFSR emerged from the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union) as the Russian Federation. Following [a constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Russian_constitutional_crisis) in 1993, [a new constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Russia) was adopted, establishing a [semi-presidential republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential_system). Since the turn of the century, [Russia's political system](25704.md) has been dominated by [Vladimir Putin](32817.md), under [whose leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin) it has experienced [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding), transforming into an [authoritarian regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism). Russia [has been militarily involved](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_the_Russian_Federation) in [numerous conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Russia#Russian_Federation_\(1991%E2%80%93present\)), including [its war with Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) in 2008 and [its war with Ukraine](42085878.md) since 2014, the latter has involved the internationally unrecognised [annexation of Crimea](42228673.md) in 2014 and [four additional regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts) following the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md) in 2022, resulting in [a full-scale war](70149799.md).&#x20;

Russia is generally considered a [great power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_power) and [wields significant regional influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power), possessing the [largest stockpile of nuclear weapons](588941.md) and having the [third-highest military expenditure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_military_expenditures) in the world. [Its economy](25705.md) ranks among the largest in the world by [PPP-adjusted GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)), relying on [its vast mineral and energy resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_as_an_energy_superpower), mainly [oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production) and [natural gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_production). Russia continues to [rank very low](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Russia) in measurements of [democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy), [human rights](785595.md) and [media freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_freedom_in_Russia), while [having high levels of corruption](27352290.md). As the [successor state of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession,_continuity_and_legacy_of_the_USSR), it [retains its seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_and_the_United_Nations) as a [permanent member of the United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_members_of_the_United_Nations_Security_Council) and is a member state of [several international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Russia#International_membership). Russia is also home to [32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Russia) UNESCO [World Heritage Sites](44940.md).&#x20;

## Etymology

Main article: [Names of Rus', Russia and Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Names_of_Rus%27,_Russia_and_Ruthenia)

According to the *[Oxford English Dictionary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_English_Dictionary)*, the English name *Russia* first appeared in the 14th century, borrowed from [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin_language): *Russia*, used in the 11th century and frequently in 12th-century British sources, in turn derived from *Russi*, 'the Russians' and the suffix *[-ia](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/-ia#Latin)*.[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]&#x20;

There are several words in Russian which translate to "Russians" in English. The noun and adjective русский, *russkiy* refers to ethnic [Russians](59510.md). The adjective российский, *rossiiskiy* denotes [Russian citizens](17005363.md) regardless of ethnicity. The same applies to the more recently coined noun россиянин, *rossiianyn*, in the sense of citizen of the Russian state.[^cite_note-Hellberg-Hirn-1998-21][^cite_note-22]&#x20;

The oldest [endonyms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endonym_and_exonym) used were *Rus'* (Русь) and the "Russian land" (Русская земля, *Russkaya zemlya*).[^cite_note-23] According to the *[Primary Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_Chronicle)*, the word *Rus'* is derived from the [Rus' people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rus%27_people), who were a [Swedish](29380512.md) tribe, and from where the three original members of the [Rurikid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurikid) dynasty came from.[^cite_note-24] The [Finnish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_language) word for Swedes, *ruotsi*, has the same origin.[^cite_note-25] In modern historiography, the early medieval [East Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) state is usually referred to as *[Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27)*, named after its capital city.[^cite_note-\:1-26] Another Medieval Latin name for *Rus'* was *[Ruthenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruthenia)*.[^cite_note-27]&#x20;

In Russian, the current name of the country, Россия (*Rossiya*), comes from the [Byzantine Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Greek) name Ρωσία (*Rosía*).[^cite_note-28] The name Росия (*Rosiya*) was first attested in 1387.[^cite_note-29] The name *Rossiya* appeared in Russian sources in the 15th century and began to replace the vernacular *Rus'* during the rise of Moscow as the centre of a unified Russian state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBushkovitch201137"Precisely_at_this_time_in_written_usage_the_modern_term_''Rossia''_(a_literary_expression_borrowed_from_Greek)_began_to_edge_out_the_traditional_and_vernacular_''Rus''"-30] However, until the end of the 17th century, the country was more often referred to by its inhabitants as *Rus'*, the "Russian land" (*Russkaya zemlya*), or the "Muscovite state" (*Moskovskoye gosudarstvo*), among other variations.[^cite_note-\:0-31][^cite_note-Hellberg-Hirn-1998-21]&#x20;

In 1721, [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great) proclaimed the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) (*Rossiyskaya imperiya*).[^cite_note-\:0-31] The name *Rossiya* was used as the common designation for the multinational Russian Empire and then for the modern Russian state.[^cite_note-\:7-32] *Rossiya* is distinguished from the ethnonym *russkiy*, as it refers to a supranational identity, including ethnic Russians.[^cite_note-\:7-32] After the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) and the proclamation of the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) in 1918, the "Russian" in the title of the state was *Rossiyskaya*, rather than *Russkaya*, as the former denoted a multinational state, while the latter had ethnic dimensions.[^cite_note-33] In modern Russian, the name *Rus'* is still used in poetry or prose to refer to either the older Russia or an imagined essence of Russia.[^cite_note-\:1-26]&#x20;

## History

Main article: [History of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Russia)

### Early history

Further information: [Ancient Greek colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_colonies), [Early Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs), [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huns), [Turkic expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_expansion), and [Prehistory of Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Siberia)

See also: [Proto-Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans) and [Proto-Uralic homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Uralic_homeland)

The earliest evidence of human settlement in what is now Russia dates to the [Oldowan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldowan) period during the early [Lower Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Paleolithic). About 2 million years ago, representatives of *[Homo erectus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_erectus)* migrated to the [Taman Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taman_Peninsula) in southern Russia.[^cite_note-34] [Flint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flint) tools, some 1.5 million years old, have been discovered in the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus).[^cite_note-35] [Radiocarbon dated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiocarbon_dated) specimens from [Denisova Cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisova_Cave) in the [Altai Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altai_Mountains) estimate the oldest [Denisovan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovan) specimen lived 195–122,700 years ago.[^cite_note-36] Fossils of *[Denny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_\(hybrid_hominin\))*, an [archaic human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaic_human) hybrid that was half [Neanderthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neanderthal) and half Denisovan, and lived some 90,000 years ago, was also found within the latter cave.[^cite_note-37] Russia was home to some of the last surviving Neanderthals, from about 45,000 years ago, found in [Mezmaiskaya cave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mezmaiskaya_cave).[^cite_note-38]&#x20;

The first trace of an [early modern human](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ust%27-Ishim_man) in Russia dates back to 45,000 years, in [Western Siberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Siberia).[^cite_note-39] The discovery of high concentration cultural remains of [anatomically modern humans](682482.md), from at least 40,000 years ago, was found at [Kostyonki–Borshchyovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kostyonki%E2%80%93Borshchyovo),[^cite_note-40] and at [Sungir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sungir), dating back to 34,600 years ago—both in [western Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Russia).[^cite_note-41] Humans reached [Arctic Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far_North_\(Russia\)) at least 40,000 years ago, in [Mamontovaya Kurya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamontovaya_Kurya).[^cite_note-42] [Ancient North Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_North_Eurasian) populations from Siberia genetically similar to [Mal'ta–Buret' culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mal%27ta%E2%80%93Buret%27_culture) and [Afontova Gora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afontova_Gora) were an important genetic contributor to [Ancient Native Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Beringian) and [Eastern Hunter-Gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Hunter-Gatherer).[^cite_note-43]&#x20;



Bronze Age spread of [Yamnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamnaya_culture) [Steppe pastoralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Steppe_Herders) ancestry between 3300 and 1500 BC,

[^cite_note-44]

&#x20;including the [Afanasievo culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasievo_culture) of southern Siberia

The [Kurgan hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurgan_hypothesis) places the Volga-Dnieper region of southern Russia and [Ukraine](31750.md) as the [urheimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urheimat) of the [Proto-Indo-Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Indo-Europeans).[^cite_note-45] Early [Indo-European migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_migrations) from the [Pontic–Caspian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic%E2%80%93Caspian_steppe) of Ukraine and Russia spread [Yamnaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yamnaya_culture) ancestry and [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_languages) across large parts of Eurasia.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] [Nomadic pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic_pastoralism) developed in the Pontic–Caspian steppe beginning in the [Chalcolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcolithic).[^cite_note-Belinskij-1999-48] Remnants of these steppe civilisations were discovered in places such as [Ipatovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ipatovo_kurgan),[^cite_note-Belinskij-1999-48] [Sintashta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sintashta),[^cite_note-mounted-49] [Arkaim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkaim),[^cite_note-50] and [Pazyryk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pazyryk_burials),[^cite_note-51] which bear the earliest known traces of [horses in warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horses_in_warfare).[^cite_note-mounted-49] The genetic makeup of speakers of the [Uralic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_language_family) language family in northern Europe was shaped by migration from [Siberia](29639.md) that began at least 3,500 years ago.[^cite_note-52]&#x20;

In the 3rd to 4th centuries AD, the [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths) kingdom of [Oium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oium) existed in southern Russia, which was later overrun by [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huns). Between the 3rd and 6th centuries AD, the [Bosporan Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bosporan_Kingdom), which was a Hellenistic [polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polity) that succeeded the Greek colonies,[^cite_note-53] was also overwhelmed by nomadic invasions led by warlike tribes such as the Huns and [Eurasian Avars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Avars).[^cite_note-54] The [Khazars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khazars), who were of [Turkic origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples), ruled the steppes between the Caucasus in the south, to the east past the Volga river basin, and west as far as [Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiev) on the Dnieper river until the 10th century.[^cite_note-55] After them came the [Pechenegs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechenegs) who created a large confederacy, which was subsequently taken over by the [Cumans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumans) and the [Kipchaks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kipchaks).[^cite_note-56]&#x20;

The ancestors of [Russians](59510.md) are among the [Slavic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_ancient_Slavic_peoples) that separated from the Proto-Indo-Europeans, who appeared in the northeastern part of Europe c. 1500 years ago.[^cite_note-57] The [East Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Slavs) gradually settled western Russia (approximately between modern [Moscow](19004.md) and [Saint-Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Petersburg)) in two waves: one moving from Kiev towards present-day [Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzdal) and [Murom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murom) and another from [Polotsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polotsk) towards [Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod) and [Rostov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rostov,_Yaroslavl_Oblast).[^cite_note-58] Prior to Slavic migration, that territory was populated by [Finno-Ugrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finno-Ugrian) peoples. From the 7th century onwards, the incoming East Slavs slowly assimilated the native Finno-Ugrians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59][^cite_note-60]&#x20;

### Kievan Rus'

Main articles: [Rus' Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rus%27_Khaganate); [Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27); and [List of tribes and states in Belarus, Russia and Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tribes_and_states_in_Belarus,_Russia_and_Ukraine)



[Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kievan_Rus%27) after the [Council of Liubech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Liubech) in 1097

The establishment of the first East Slavic states in the 9th century coincided with the arrival of the [Varangians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varangians), [Vikings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vikings) who ventured along the waterways extending from the eastern Baltic [to the Black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/From_the_Varangians_to_the_Greeks) and [Caspian Seas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga_trade_route).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19986Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-61][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardThompson20215–6-62] According to the *[Primary Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_Chronicle)*, a Varangian from the [Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rus%27_people), named [Rurik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurik), was elected ruler of [Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod) in 862.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes19987-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChannonHudson19958-64] In 882, his successor [Oleg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleg_of_Novgorod) ventured south and conquered Kiev, which had been previously paying tribute to the [Khazars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khazars).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19986Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-61][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes19987–8-65] Rurik's son [Igor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_of_Kiev) and Igor's son [Sviatoslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sviatoslav_I_of_Kiev) subsequently subdued all local East Slavic tribes to Kievan rule, destroyed the Khazar state, and launched several military expeditions to [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sviatoslav%27s_invasion_of_Bulgaria), [Byzantium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paphlagonian_expedition_of_the_Rus%27) and [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caspian_expeditions_of_the_Rus%27).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes19989–10-66][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaleotti202439–40-67][^cite_note-68]&#x20;

During the 10th and 11th centuries, Kievan Rus' became one of the largest and most prosperous states in Europe. The reigns of [Vladimir the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_the_Great) (980–1015) and his son [Yaroslav the Wise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslav_the_Wise) (1019–1054) constitute Kiev's [Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19986–7Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-69] Through his political and religious reforms, Vladimir laid the foundations for the transformation of Kievan Rus' from a fragmented conglomeration of tributary groups into a more unified realm, connected by dynastic authority and shared religious and cultural ties.[^cite_note-70] He [adopted Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianisation_of_Kievan_Rus%27) from the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire), beginning the synthesis of Byzantine and Slavic cultures that would define Russian culture for the next millennium.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19983,_5–7Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-71][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChannonHudson199526-72] The reign of Yaroslav also saw the creation of the state's first written legal code, the *[Russkaya Pravda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russkaya_Pravda)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19987Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-73]&#x20;

Kievan Rus' was politically unstable due to weak centralisation and the absence of a stable succession principle.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardThompson202119-74] The [rota system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rota_system) introduced by Yaroslav led to princely authority being distributed among members of the ruling [Rurikid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurikid) dynasty according to seniority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardThompson202119-74] Intended to preserve dynastic unity, this system increasingly fostered rivalry among princes, leading to frequent infighting.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardThompson202119-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19989Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-75] As a result, political power [fragmented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudal_fragmentation) among competing regional centres, marking a gradual shift [toward decentralisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disintegration_of_Kievan_Rus%27).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19989Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-75][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWardThompson202119–20-76][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBushkovitch201113–14-77] Kiev's dominance waned, to the benefit of [Vladimir-Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir-Suzdal) in the north-east, the [Novgorod Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Republic) in the north, and [Galicia–Volhynia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galicia%E2%80%93Volhynia) in the south-west.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19989–10Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-78][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes199814-79] By the 12th century, Kiev lost its pre-eminence and Kievan Rus' had fragmented into different principalities.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChannonHudson199516-80] Prince [Andrey Bogolyubsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Bogolyubsky) sacked Kiev in 1169 and made [Vladimir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir,_Russia) his base,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEChannonHudson199516-80] leading to political power being shifted to the north-east.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19989–10Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-78]&#x20;

Vladimir-Suzdal continued the tradition of strong princely rule, while the Novgorod Republic, which formally won its independence in 1136, was an exception.[^cite_note-81] From the mid-13th century, the throne of Novgorod was held by the grand princes of Vladimir, although the prince's authority gradually diminished in favour of more republican-style governance, with political power increasingly held by the *[veche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veche)* (popular assembly) and elected officials.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBushkovitch201124-82][^cite_note-83]<sup>: 83 </sup> Novgorod became a major commercial centre through the fur trade and an important centre of Russian culture.[^cite_note-84] Led by Prince [Alexander Nevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Nevsky), the Novgorodians repelled the invading [Swedes](29380512.md) in the [Battle of the Neva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Neva) in 1240, as well as the [Germanic crusaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Crusades) in the [Battle on the Ice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_on_the_Ice) in 1242.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes199826-85][^cite_note-86]&#x20;

As it was undergoing fragmentation, Kievan Rus' finally fell to the [Mongol invasions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Kievan_Rus%27) of 1237–1238, which overran much of the region and later resulted in the [sacking of Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Kiev_\(1240\)) in 1240 and other cities, as well as the death of a significant portion of the population.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199810Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-87] The invaders, later known as [Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars), formed the state of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde), which ruled over Russia for the next two centuries.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDukes199826–27-88][^cite_note-89] Only the Novgorod Republic escaped foreign occupation after it agreed to pay tribute to the Mongols.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199810Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-87] Galicia–Volhynia would later be absorbed by [Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania) and [Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Poland_\(1025%E2%80%931385\)), while the Novgorod Republic continued to prosper in the north.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis19989–10Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-78] In the northeast, the Byzantine-Slavic traditions of Kievan Rus' were adapted to form the Russian autocratic state.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199811Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-90]&#x20;

### Grand Principality of Moscow

Main article: [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow)

Further information: [Appanage Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appanage_Russia)



[Sergius of Radonezh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergius_of_Radonezh) blessing [Dmitry Donskoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Donskoy) in [Trinity Sergius Lavra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Sergius_Lavra), before the [Battle of Kulikovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kulikovo), depicted in a painting by [Ernst Lissner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Lissner)

The destruction of Kievan Rus' saw the eventual rise of the [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow), initially a part of [Vladimir-Suzdal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir-Suzdal).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59]<sup>: 11–20 </sup> While still under the domain of the [Mongol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol)-[Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatars) and with their connivance, Moscow began to assert its influence in the region in the early 14th century,[^cite_note-91] gradually becoming the leading force in the "[gathering of the Russian lands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gathering_of_the_Russian_lands)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199811–12Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-92][^cite_note-93] When the seat of the metropolitan of the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) moved to Moscow in 1325, its influence increased.[^cite_note-94] Moscow's last rival, the [Novgorod Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Republic), prospered as the chief [fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fur_trade) centre and the easternmost port of the [Hanseatic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanseatic_League).[^cite_note-95]&#x20;

Led by Prince [Dmitry Donskoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Donskoy) of Moscow, the united army of [Russian principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_principalities) inflicted [a milestone defeat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_in_Eastern_Europe_during_Turco-Mongol_rule) on the Mongol-Tatars in the [Battle of Kulikovo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kulikovo) in 1380.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199810Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGaleotti202484-96] Moscow gradually absorbed its parent duchy and surrounding principalities, including formerly strong rivals such as [Tver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Tver) and [Novgorod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Republic).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199811–12Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-92]&#x20;

[Ivan III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_III) ("the Great") threw off the control of the [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) and gained sovereignty over the ethnically Russian lands;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199812Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-97] he later adopted the title of [sovereign of all Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_of_all_Russia).[^cite_note-98] After the [fall of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople) in 1453, Moscow [claimed succession to the legacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Rome) of the [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman_Empire). Ivan III married [Sophia Palaiologina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophia_Palaiologina), the niece of the last [Byzantine emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_emperor) [Constantine XI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_XI), and made the Byzantine [double-headed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double-headed_eagle) his own, and eventually Russia's, coat-of-arms.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis199812Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-97] [Vasili III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasili_III_of_Russia) united all of Russia by annexing the last few independent [Russian states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tribes_and_states_in_Belarus,_Russia_and_Ukraine) in the early 16th century.[^cite_note-99]&#x20;

### Tsardom of Russia

Main article: [Tsardom of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsardom_of_Russia)

See also: [Moscow, third Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow,_third_Rome)



[Ivan IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_IV) was the [Grand Prince of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Prince_of_Moscow) from 1533 to 1547, then [Tsar of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar_of_Russia) until his death in 1584.

In development of the [Third Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow,_third_Rome) ideas, [Ivan IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_IV) ("the Terrible") was officially crowned as the first [tsar of all Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsar_of_all_Russia) in 1547. The tsar [promulgated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promulgation) a new code of laws ([Sudebnik of 1550](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudebnik_of_1550)), established the first Russian feudal representative body (the [Zemsky Sobor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zemsky_Sobor)), revamped the military, curbed the influence of the clergy, and reorganised local government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] During his long reign, Ivan nearly doubled the already large Russian territory by annexing the three Tatar khanates: [Kazan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Kazan) and [Astrakhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astrakhan_Khanate) along the [Volga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volga),[^cite_note-100] and the [Khanate of Sibir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khanate_of_Sibir) in southwestern Siberia. Ultimately, by the end of the 16th century, Russia expanded east of the [Ural Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ural_Mountains).[^cite_note-101] However, the Tsardom was weakened by the long and unsuccessful [Livonian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livonian_War) against the coalition of the [Kingdom of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_of_the_Kingdom_of_Poland) and the [Grand Duchy of Lithuania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Duchy_of_Lithuania) (later the united [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth)), the [Kingdom of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Sweden_\(1523%E2%80%931611\)), and [Denmark–Norway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denmark%E2%80%93Norway) for access to the Baltic coast and sea trade.[^cite_note-102] In 1571, the [Crimean Tatars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate), supported by the [Ottomans](22278.md), [burned down Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_of_Moscow_\(1571\)), destroying everything except the Kremlin. The following year, the Crimeans attempted another raid on Moscow, but this time they were defeated in the crucial [Battle of Molodi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Molodi).[^cite_note-103]&#x20;



Feodor Godunov's map of Russia, as published by [Hessel Gerritsz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hessel_Gerritsz) in 1614

The death of Ivan's sons marked the end of the ancient [Rurik dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rurik_dynasty) in 1598, and in combination with the [famine of 1601–1603](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_famine_of_1601%E2%80%9303), led to a civil war, the rule of pretenders, and foreign intervention during the [Time of Troubles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_of_Troubles) in the early 17th century.[^cite_note-104] The [Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Lithuanian_Commonwealth), taking advantage, occupied parts of Russia, extending into the capital Moscow.[^cite_note-105] In 1612, the Poles were forced to retreat by the Russian volunteer corps, led by merchant [Kuzma Minin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuzma_Minin) and prince [Dmitry Pozharsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Pozharsky).[^cite_note-106] The [Romanov dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanov_dynasty) acceded to the throne in 1613 by the decision of the Zemsky Sobor, and the country started its gradual recovery from the crisis.[^cite_note-107]&#x20;

Russia continued its territorial growth through the 17th century, which was the age of the [Cossacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cossacks).[^cite_note-Siberia-108] In 1654, the Ukrainian leader, [Bohdan Khmelnytsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohdan_Khmelnytsky), offered to place Ukraine under the protection of the Russian tsar, [Alexis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexis_of_Russia), whose acceptance of this offer led to another [Russo-Polish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Polish_War_\(1654%E2%80%931667\)). Ultimately, Ukraine was split along the [Dnieper](380138.md), placing [left-bank Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-bank_Ukraine) and Kiev under Russian rule.[^cite_note-109] In the east, the rapid Russian exploration and colonisation of vast Siberia continued, hunting for valuable furs and ivory. [Russian explorers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_explorers) pushed eastward primarily along the [Siberian River Routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_River_Routes), and by the mid-17th century, there were Russian settlements in eastern Siberia, on the [Chukchi Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chukchi_Peninsula), along the [Amur River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amur_River), and on the coast of the Pacific Ocean.[^cite_note-Siberia-108] In 1648, [Semyon Dezhnyov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semyon_Dezhnyov) became the first European to navigate through the [Bering Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Strait).[^cite_note-110]&#x20;

### Imperial Russia

Main article: [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire)

Under [Peter the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great), Russia was proclaimed an empire in 1721, and established itself as one of the European great powers. Ruling from 1682 to 1725, Peter defeated Sweden in the [Great Northern War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Northern_War) (1700–1721), securing Russia's access to the sea and sea trade. In 1703, Peter founded [Saint Petersburg](24320051.md) on the Baltic Sea by establishing the [Peter and Paul Fortress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_and_Paul_Fortress) as a strategic outpost; the city later replaced Moscow as Russia's capital in 1712. Throughout his rule, [sweeping reforms were made](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_reform_of_Peter_the_Great), which brought significant Western European cultural influences to Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] He was succeeded by [Catherine I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_I_of_Russia) (1725–1727), followed by [Peter II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_II_of_Russia) (1727–1730), and [Anna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_of_Russia). The reign of Peter I's daughter [Elizabeth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabeth_of_Russia) from 1741 to 1762 saw Russia's participation in the [Seven Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War) (1756–1763). During the conflict, Russian troops overran [East Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Prussia), reaching Berlin.[^cite_note-111] However, upon Elizabeth's death, all these conquests were returned to the [Kingdom of Prussia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia) by pro-Prussian [Peter III of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_III_of_Russia).[^cite_note-112]&#x20;



[Expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_of_Russia_\(1500%E2%80%931800\)) and [territorial evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territorial_evolution_of_Russia) of Russia from the [coronation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_the_Russian_monarch) of [Ivan IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_the_Terrible) to the death of [Peter I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_the_Great)

[Catherine II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_the_Great) ("the Great"), who ruled from 1762 to 1796, presided over the [Russian Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Enlightenment). She extended Russian political control over the Polish–Lithuanian Commonwealth and [annexed most of its territories into Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partitions_of_Poland), making it the most populous country in Europe.[^cite_note-113] In the south, after the successful [Russo-Turkish Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_Wars) against the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), Catherine advanced Russia's boundary to the Black Sea, by dissolving the [Crimean Khanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_Khanate), and [annexing Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Empire).[^cite_note-114] As a result of victories over [Qajar Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qajar_dynasty) through the [Russo-Persian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Persian_Wars), by the first half of the 19th century, Russia also [conquered the Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_conquest_of_the_Caucasus).[^cite_note-115] Catherine's successor, her son [Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_I_of_Russia), was [unstable and focused predominantly on domestic issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personality_and_reputation_of_Paul_I_of_Russia).[^cite_note-116] Following his short reign, Catherine's strategy was continued with [Alexander I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia)'s (1801–1825) [wresting of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finnish_War) from the weakened Sweden in 1809,[^cite_note-117] and of [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia) from the Ottomans in 1812.[^cite_note-118] In North America, the Russians became the first Europeans to [reach and colonise Alaska](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_America).[^cite_note-119] In 1803–1806, the [first Russian circumnavigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Russian_circumnavigation) was made.[^cite_note-120] In 1820, [a Russian expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fabian_Gottlieb_von_Bellingshausen#First_Russian_Antarctic_expedition) discovered the continent of [Antarctica](18959138.md).[^cite_note-121]&#x20;

#### Great power and development of society, sciences, and arts



*[Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon)'s retreat from Moscow* by [Albrecht Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albrecht_Adam) (1851)

During the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars), Russia joined alliances with various European powers, and fought against France. The [French invasion of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_invasion_of_Russia) at the height of Napoleon's power in 1812 reached Moscow, but eventually failed as the obstinate resistance in combination with the bitterly cold [Russian winter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_winter) led to a disastrous defeat of invaders, in which the pan-European [Grande Armée](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grande_Arm%C3%A9e) faced utter destruction. Led by [Mikhail Kutuzov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Kutuzov) and [Michael Andreas Barclay de Tolly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Andreas_Barclay_de_Tolly), the [Imperial Russian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_Russian_Army) ousted Napoleon and drove throughout Europe in the [War of the Sixth Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_of_the_Sixth_Coalition), ultimately entering Paris.[^cite_note-122] [Alexander I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_I_of_Russia) controlled Russia's delegation at the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna), which defined the map of post-Napoleonic Europe.[^cite_note-123]&#x20;

The officers who pursued Napoleon into Western Europe brought ideas of liberalism back to Russia, and attempted to curtail the tsar's powers during the abortive [Decembrist revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decembrist_revolt) of 1825.[^cite_note-124] At the end of the conservative reign of [Nicholas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_I_of_Russia) (1825–1855), a zenith period of Russia's power and influence in Europe, was disrupted by defeat in the [Crimean War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimean_War).[^cite_note-125]&#x20;

#### Great liberal reforms and capitalism



The [Battle of Shipka Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Shipka_Pass) for the control of the vital [Shipka Pass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shipka_Pass) during the [1877–1878 Russo-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1877%E2%80%931878\))

Nicholas' successor [Alexander II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_II_of_Russia) (1855–1881) enacted significant changes throughout the country, including the [emancipation reform of 1861](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emancipation_reform_of_1861).[^cite_note-126] These reforms spurred industrialisation, and modernised the Imperial Russian Army, which liberated much of the [Balkans](4829.md) from Ottoman rule in the aftermath of the [1877–1878 Russo-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1877%E2%80%931878\)).[^cite_note-127] During most of the 19th and early 20th centuries, Russia and [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire) colluded over [Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Afghanistan) and its neighbouring territories in [Central](6742.md) and South Asia; the rivalry between the two major European empires came to be known as the [Great Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Game).[^cite_note-128]&#x20;

The late 19th century saw the rise of various socialist movements in Russia. Alexander II was [assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Alexander_II_of_Russia) in 1881 by revolutionary terrorists.[^cite_note-129] The reign of his son [Alexander III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_III_of_Russia) (1881–1894) was less liberal but more peaceful.[^cite_note-130]&#x20;

#### Constitutional monarchy and World War

During the reign of the last Russian emperor, [Nicholas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II) (1894–1917), the [Revolution of 1905](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_1905) was precipitated by the disastrous and humiliating defeat in the [Russo-Japanese War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Japanese_War).[^cite_note-131] The uprising was put down, but the government was forced to concede major reforms ([Russian Constitution of 1906](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Constitution_of_1906)), including granting [freedoms of speech](21401843.md) and [assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_assembly), the legalisation of political parties, and the creation of an elected legislative body, the [State Duma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Duma_\(Russian_Empire\)).[^cite_note-132]&#x20;

### Revolution and civil war

Main articles: [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution), [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War), and [Dissolution of the Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Russian_Empire)



Emperor [Nicholas II of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia) and the [Romanovs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Romanov) were [executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Execution_of_the_Romanov_family) by the Bolsheviks in 1918.

In 1914, [Russia entered World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_entry_into_World_War_I) in response to [Austria-Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria-Hungary)'s declaration of war on Russia's ally [Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Serbia),[^cite_note-133] and fought across multiple fronts while isolated from its [Triple Entente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Entente) allies.[^cite_note-134] In 1916, the [Brusilov Offensive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusilov_Offensive) of the Imperial Russian Army almost completely destroyed the [Austro-Hungarian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austro-Hungarian_Army).[^cite_note-135] However, the already-existing public distrust of the regime was deepened by the rising costs of war, [high casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I_casualties), and rumours of corruption and treason. All this formed the climate for the [Russian Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revolution) of 1917, carried out in two major acts.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] In early 1917, [Nicholas II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_II_of_Russia) was [forced to abdicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/February_Revolution); he and his family were imprisoned and [later executed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shooting_of_the_Romanov_family) during the [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War).[^cite_note-136] The monarchy was replaced by a shaky coalition of political parties that declared itself the [Provisional Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Provisional_Government),[^cite_note-137] and proclaimed the [Russian Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Republic). On 19 January \[[O.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Style_and_New_Style_dates) 6 January], 1918, the [Russian Constituent Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Constituent_Assembly) declared Russia a democratic federal republic (thus ratifying the Provisional Government's decision). The next day the Constituent Assembly was dissolved by the [All-Russian Central Executive Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-Russian_Central_Executive_Committee).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59]&#x20;

An alternative socialist establishment co-existed, the [Petrograd Soviet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrograd_Soviet), wielding power through the democratically elected councils of workers and peasants, called *[soviets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_\(council\))*. The rule of the new authorities only aggravated the crisis in the country instead of resolving it, and eventually, the [October Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/October_Revolution), led by [Bolshevik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshevik) leader [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin), overthrew the Provisional Government and gave full governing power to the soviets, leading to the creation of the world's first [socialist state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_state).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] The [Russian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Civil_War) broke out between the [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communist) [White movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_movement) and the Bolsheviks with its [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army).[^cite_note-138] In the aftermath of signing the [Treaty of Brest-Litovsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Brest-Litovsk) that concluded hostilities with the [Central Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Powers) of [World War I](4764461.md), Bolshevist Russia surrendered most of its western territories, which hosted 34% of its population, 54% of its industries, 32% of its agricultural land, and roughly 90% of its coal mines.[^cite_note-139]&#x20;



[Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin) speaks in Moscow, 1920, with [Leon Trotsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Trotsky) leaning against the podium.

The [Allied powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) launched an unsuccessful [military intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allied_intervention_in_the_Russian_Civil_War) in support of anti-communist forces.[^cite_note-140] In the meantime, both the Bolsheviks and White movement carried out campaigns of deportations and executions against each other, known respectively as the [Red Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Terror) and [White Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Terror_\(Russia\)).[^cite_note-141] By the end of the violent civil war, Russia's economy and infrastructure were heavily damaged, and as many as 10 million perished during the war, mostly civilians.[^cite_note-142] Millions became [White émigrés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_%C3%A9migr%C3%A9),[^cite_note-143] and the [Russian famine of 1921–1922](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_famine_of_1921%E2%80%931922) claimed up to five million victims.[^cite_note-144]&#x20;

### Soviet Union

Main article: [History of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Soviet_Union)



Location of the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) (red) within the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) in 1936

#### Command economy and Soviet society

On 30 December 1922, Lenin and his aides [formed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_on_the_Creation_of_the_USSR) the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union), by joining the [Russian SFSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) into a single state with the [Byelorussian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byelorussian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic), [Transcaucasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transcaucasian_Socialist_Federative_Soviet_Republic), and [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_Soviet_Socialist_Republic) republics.[^cite_note-145] Eventually, internal border changes and annexations during World War II resulted in a union of [15 republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republics_of_the_Soviet_Union), the largest and most populous being the Russian SFSR, which dominated the union politically, culturally, and economically.[^cite_note-146]&#x20;

Following [Lenin's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Vladimir_Lenin) in 1924, a [troika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Troikas_in_the_Soviet_Union) was designated to take charge. Eventually [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin), the [General Secretary of the Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Secretary_of_the_Communist_Party_of_the_Soviet_Union), managed to suppress all opposition factions and consolidate power in his hands to become the country's dictator by the 1930s.[^cite_note-147] [Leon Trotsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Trotsky), the main proponent of [world revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_revolution), was exiled from the Soviet Union in 1929,[^cite_note-148] and Stalin's idea of [Socialism in One Country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_in_One_Country) became the official line.[^cite_note-149] The continued internal struggle in the Bolshevik party culminated in the [Great Purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Purge).[^cite_note-150]&#x20;

#### Stalinism and modernisation



Congratulations sent by [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin) on the opening of the [Stalingrad Tractor Plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volgograd_Tractor_Plant)

Under Stalin's leadership, the government launched a [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_economy), [industrialisation of the largely rural country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrialization_in_the_USSR), and [collectivisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collectivization_in_the_USSR) of [its agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_the_USSR). During this period of rapid economic and social change, millions of people were sent to [penal labour camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag), including many political convicts for their suspected or real opposition to Stalin's rule,[^cite_note-151] and millions were [deported and exiled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_transfer_in_the_Soviet_Union) to remote areas of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-152] The transitional disorganisation of the country's agriculture, combined with the harsh state policies and a drought,[^cite_note-153] led to the [Soviet famine of 1932–1933](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_famine_of_1932%E2%80%931933), which killed 5.7[^cite_note-154] to 8.7 million, 3.3 million of them in the Russian SFSR.[^cite_note-155] The Soviet Union underwent a rapid and costly transformation from a predominantly agrarian economy into a major industrial power.[^cite_note-156]&#x20;

#### World War II and United Nations

Main article: [Soviet Union in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_in_World_War_II)



Two teenage girls assemble [PPD-40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PPD-40) submachine guns during the [Siege of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leningrad) in 1942.



The [Battle of Stalingrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stalingrad), the largest and bloodiest battle in the history of warfare, ended in 1943 with a decisive Soviet victory against the [German army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Army_\(1935%E2%80%931945\)).

The Soviet Union entered [World War II](32927.md) on 17 September 1939 with its [invasion of Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Poland),[^cite_note-157] in accordance with a secret protocol within the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact) with [Nazi Germany](21212.md).[^cite_note-158] The Soviet Union later [invaded Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_War),[^cite_note-159] and [occupied and annexed the Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_the_Baltic_states_\(1940\)),[^cite_note-160] as well as [parts of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Bessarabia_and_Northern_Bukovina).[^cite_note-161]<sup>: 91–95 </sup> On 22 June 1941, Germany [invaded the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Barbarossa),[^cite_note-162] opening the [Eastern Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Front_\(World_War_II\)), the largest theatre of World War II.[^cite_note-163]<sup>: 7 </sup>&#x20;

Eventually, some 5 million [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) troops were captured by the Nazis;[^cite_note-164]<sup>: 272 </sup> the latter deliberately [starved to death or otherwise killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_mistreatment_of_Soviet_prisoners_of_war) 3.3 million Soviet [POWs](25008.md), and a vast number of civilians, as the "[Hunger Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_Plan)" sought to fulfil [Generalplan Ost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalplan_Ost).[^cite_note-165]<sup>: 175–186 </sup> Although the [Wehrmacht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wehrmacht) had considerable early success, their attack was halted in the [Battle of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Moscow).[^cite_note-166] Subsequently, the Germans were dealt major defeats first at the [Battle of Stalingrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Stalingrad) in the winter of 1942–1943,[^cite_note-167] and then in the [Battle of Kursk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kursk) in the summer of 1943.[^cite_note-168] Another German failure was the [Siege of Leningrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Leningrad), in which the city was fully blockaded on land between 1941 and 1944 by German and Finnish forces, and suffered starvation and more than a million deaths, but never surrendered.[^cite_note-169] Soviet forces steamrolled through Eastern and Central Europe in 1944–1945 and [captured Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Berlin) in May 1945.[^cite_note-170] In August 1945, the Red Army [invaded Manchuria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_invasion_of_Manchuria) and [ousted the Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Japanese_War) from Northeast Asia, contributing to the Allied victory over Japan.[^cite_note-171]&#x20;

The 1941–1945 period of World War II is known in Russia as the [Great Patriotic War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Patriotic_War_\(term\)).[^cite_note-172] The Soviet Union, along with the United States, the United Kingdom and China were considered the Big Four of Allied powers in World War II, and later became the [Four Policemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Policemen), which was the foundation of the [United Nations Security Council](31956.md).[^cite_note-173]<sup>: 27 </sup> During the war, [Soviet civilian and military death were about 26–27 million](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties_of_the_Soviet_Union),[^cite_note-174] accounting for about half of all [World War II casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II_casualties).[^cite_note-175]<sup>: 295 </sup> The [Soviet economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Soviet_Union) and infrastructure suffered massive devastation, which caused the [Soviet famine of 1946–1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_famine_of_1946%E2%80%931947).[^cite_note-176] However, at the expense of a large sacrifice, the Soviet Union emerged as a superpower.[^cite_note-Reiman-2016-177]&#x20;

#### Superpower and Cold War



The "[Big Three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Alliance_\(World_War_II\))" at the [Yalta Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yalta_Conference) in February 1945, [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill), [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) and [Joseph Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Stalin)

After World War II, according to the [Potsdam Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potsdam_Conference), the [Red Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Army) occupied parts of Eastern and Central Europe, including [East Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Germany) and the eastern regions of [Austria](26964606.md).[^cite_note-178] Dependent communist governments were installed in the [Eastern Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Bloc) satellite states.[^cite_note-179] After becoming the world's second [nuclear power](588941.md),[^cite_note-180] the Soviet Union established the [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) alliance,[^cite_note-181] and entered into a struggle for global dominance, known as the [Cold War](325329.md), with the rivalling United States and [NATO](21133.md).[^cite_note-182]&#x20;

#### Khrushchev Thaw reforms and economic development

After [Stalin's death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Joseph_Stalin) in 1953 and a short period of [collective leadership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collective_leadership_in_the_Soviet_Union), the new leader [Nikita Khrushchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikita_Khrushchev) denounced [Stalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Cult_of_Personality_and_Its_Consequences) and launched the policy of [de-Stalinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De-Stalinization), releasing many political prisoners from the [Gulag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulag) labour camps.[^cite_note-183] The general easement of repressive policies became known later as the [Khrushchev Thaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khrushchev_Thaw).[^cite_note-184] At the same time, Cold War tensions reached its peak when the two rivals clashed over the deployment of the United States [Jupiter missiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PGM-19_Jupiter) in Turkey and Soviet [missiles in Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuban_Missile_Crisis).[^cite_note-185]&#x20;

In 1957, the Soviet Union launched the world's first artificial [satellite](27683.md), *[Sputnik 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik_1)*, thus starting the [Space Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_Age).[^cite_note-186] Russian [cosmonaut](664.md) [Yuri Gagarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yuri_Gagarin) became the first human to orbit the Earth, aboard the *[Vostok 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vostok_1)* crewed spacecraft on [12 April 1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmonautics_Day).[^cite_note-187]&#x20;

#### Period of developed socialism or Era of Stagnation

Following the ousting of Khrushchev in 1964, another period of collective leadership ensued, until [Leonid Brezhnev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Brezhnev) became the leader. The era of the 1970s and the early 1980s was later designated as the [Era of Stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation). The 1965 [Kosygin reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kosygin_reform) aimed for partial [decentralisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decentralisation) of the [Soviet economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_economy).[^cite_note-188] In 1979, after a [communist-led revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saur_Revolution) in Afghanistan, Soviet forces invaded the country, ultimately starting the [Soviet–Afghan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet%E2%80%93Afghan_War).[^cite_note-189] In May 1988, the [Soviets started to withdraw from Afghanistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_withdrawal_from_Afghanistan), due to international opposition, persistent anti-Soviet guerrilla warfare, and a lack of support by Soviet citizens.[^cite_note-190]&#x20;

#### Perestroika, democratisation and Russian sovereignty



Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev) and US President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) in [Red Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Square) during the [Moscow Summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Summit_\(1988\)), 31 May 1988

From 1985 onwards, the last Soviet leader [Mikhail Gorbachev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev), who sought to enact liberal reforms in the Soviet system, introduced the policies of *[glasnost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glasnost)* (openness) and *[perestroika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perestroika)* (restructuring) in an attempt to end the [period of economic stagnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Era_of_Stagnation) and to [democratise the government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demokratizatsiya_\(Soviet_Union\)).[^cite_note-191] This, however, led to the rise of strong nationalist and separatist movements across the country.[^cite_note-192] Prior to 1991, the Soviet economy was the world's second-largest, but during its final years, it went into a crisis.[^cite_note-193]&#x20;

By 1991, economic and political turmoil began to boil over as the [Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states) chose to secede from the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-194] On 17 March, a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_Union_referendum) was held, in which the vast majority of participating citizens voted in favour of changing the Soviet Union into a [renewed federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Union_Treaty).[^cite_note-195] In June 1991, [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) became the first directly elected [President](239646.md) in Russian history when he was [elected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Russian_presidential_election) President of the Russian SFSR.[^cite_note-196] In August 1991, [a coup d'état attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Soviet_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_attempt) by members of Gorbachev's government, directed against Gorbachev and aimed at preserving the Soviet Union, instead led to the end of the Communist Party of the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-197] On 25 December 1991, following the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), along with contemporary Russia, fourteen other [post-Soviet states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-Soviet_states) emerged.[^cite_note-198]&#x20;

### Independent Russian Federation

Main article: [History of Russia (1991–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Russia_\(1991%E2%80%93present\))

Further information: [Presidency of Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Boris_Yeltsin), [Russia under Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_under_Vladimir_Putin), and [Presidency of Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Dmitry_Medvedev)

#### Transition to a market economy and political crises



[Vladimir Putin](32817.md) takes the oath of office as president on his [first inauguration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Vladimir_Putin), with [Boris Yeltsin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Yeltsin) looking over, 2000

The economic and political collapse of the Soviet Union led Russia into a deep and prolonged depression. During and after the disintegration of the Soviet Union, wide-ranging reforms including [privatisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privatization_in_Russia) and [market and trade liberalisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_trade) were undertaken, including radical changes along the lines of "[shock therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shock_therapy_\(economics\))".[^cite_note-199] The privatisation largely shifted control of enterprises from state agencies to individuals with inside connections in the government, which led to the rise of [Russian oligarchs](1209554.md).[^cite_note-200] Many of the newly rich moved billions in cash and assets outside of the country in an enormous [capital flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_flight).[^cite_note-201] The depression of the economy led to the collapse of social services—the [birth rate](515898.md) plummeted while the [death rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_rate) skyrocketed,[^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203] and millions plunged into poverty,[^cite_note-204] while extreme corruption,[^cite_note-205] as well as criminal gangs and organised crime rose significantly.[^cite_note-206]&#x20;

In late 1993, tensions between Yeltsin and the Russian parliament culminated in [a constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Russian_constitutional_crisis) which ended violently through military force. During the crisis, Yeltsin was backed by Western governments, and over 100 people were killed.[^cite_note-207]&#x20;

#### Modern liberal constitution, international cooperation and economic stabilisation

In December, a [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_Russian_constitutional_referendum) was held and approved, which introduced a new constitution, giving the president enormous powers.[^cite_note-208] The 1990s were plagued by armed conflicts in the [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus), both local ethnic skirmishes and separatist [Islamist](15012.md) insurrections.[^cite_note-209] From the time [Chechen](6095.md) separatists declared independence in the early 1990s, an [intermittent guerrilla war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Chechen_War) was fought between the rebel groups and Russian forces.[^cite_note-210] [Terrorist attacks against civilians](8743696.md) were carried out by Chechen separatists, claiming the lives of thousands of Russian civilians.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212]&#x20;

After the dissolution of the Soviet Union, Russia assumed responsibility for settling the latter's external debts.[^cite_note-213] In 1992, most consumer price controls were eliminated, causing extreme inflation and significantly devaluing the rouble.[^cite_note-214] High budget deficits coupled with increasing capital flight and inability to pay back debts, caused the [1998 Russian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Russian_financial_crisis), which resulted in a further GDP decline.[^cite_note-215]&#x20;

#### Movement towards a modernised economy, political centralisation and democratic backsliding

Further information: [Putinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Putinism)



President [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) with U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) after signing the [New START](26709878.md) treaty on [nuclear disarmament](22165.md) in 2010, which was suspended by Putin in 2023

[^cite_note-216]

On 31 December 1999, President Yeltsin unexpectedly resigned,[^cite_note-217] handing the post to the recently appointed prime minister and his chosen successor, [Vladimir Putin](32817.md).[^cite_note-218] Putin then won the [2000 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Russian_presidential_election),[^cite_note-219] and defeated the Chechen insurgency in the [Second Chechen War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Chechen_War).[^cite_note-220]&#x20;

Putin won a [second presidential term](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Russian_presidential_election) in 2004.[^cite_note-221] [High oil prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Price_of_petroleum) and a rise in foreign investment saw the [Russian economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_economy) and living standards improve significantly.[^cite_note-Ellyatt-2021-222] Putin's rule increased stability, while transforming Russia into an [authoritarian state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarianism#Examples).[^cite_note-223] In 2008, Putin took the post of prime minister, while [Dmitry Medvedev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Medvedev) was [elected President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russian_presidential_election) for one term, to hold onto power despite legal [term limits](505825.md);[^cite_note-224] this period has been described as a "[tandemocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medvedev%E2%80%93Putin_tandemocracy)".[^cite_note-225]&#x20;

Following [a diplomatic standoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Russo-Georgian_diplomatic_crisis) with neighbouring [Georgia](48768.md) in 2008, Russian forces [invaded the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Georgian_War) from 1–16 August 2008 and [occupied territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupied_territories_of_Georgia) that it has [since considered](19042397.md) as independent states, [Abkhazia](18933375.md) and [South Ossetia](318381.md).[^cite_note-226] The conflict marked the [first war in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_conflicts_in_Europe) in the 21st century.[^cite_note-227] The [2008 constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Russia) saw the terms of the president extend to six years and the lower house (State Duma) to five years.[^cite_note-228] Putin then went on to win the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Russian_presidential_election), which fuelled the "[Snow Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%932013_Russian_protests)" protests.[^cite_note-229]&#x20;

#### Russo-Ukrainian war and 2022 invasion

Main article: [Russo-Ukrainian war](42085878.md)



Map of Ukraine as of December 2025:

&#x20; Continuously controlled by Ukraine

&#x20; [Currently occupied or controlled by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine)

&#x20; [Formerly occupied by Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian-occupied_territories_of_Ukraine#Formerly_occupied_territories)

In 2014, following [a pro-Western revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolution_of_Dignity) in Ukraine, Russia invaded and [annexed Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_Crimea_by_the_Russian_Federation).[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231] It also supported [an insurgency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_pro-Russian_unrest_in_Ukraine) in the [Donbas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donbas) region of [eastern Ukraine](13065940.md),[^cite_note-232] and aided pro-Russian separatists waging a [war against the Ukrainian government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_in_Donbas).[^cite_note-233] The [frozen conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frozen_conflict)[^cite_note-234] escalated into [a full-scale Russian invasion of the remainder of Ukraine](79400268.md) on 24 February 2022,[^cite_note-Kofman_101-235][^cite_note-u538-236] initiating the largest [conventional war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conventional_warfare) in Europe since World War II.[^cite_note-237]&#x20;

The invasion met with [international condemnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine),[^cite_note-238] and [expanded sanctions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_sanctions_during_the_2022_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine) against Russia.[^cite_note-Walsh-2022-239] Russia was expelled from the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) in March 2022,[^cite_note-240] and [subsequently suspended](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/3) from the [United Nations Human Rights Council](635790.md) the following month.[^cite_note-241] In March 2023, the [International Criminal Court](14880.md) issued [an arrest warrant](73313153.md) for Putin on charges of [war crimes](70167888.md), and in June 2024, it issued arrest warrants for senior Russian military officials, including [Valery Gerasimov](37587470.md) and [Sergei Shoigu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Shoigu), on charges of war crimes and [crimes against humanity](61099.md).[^cite_note-242]<sup>: 96–97 </sup>[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244]&#x20;

Russia initially made rapid advances in the northern and eastern fronts, yet [failed to capture Kyiv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kyiv_\(2022\)) and overthrow the Ukrainian government, leading to a subsequent [withdrawal from the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_front_of_the_Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine).[^cite_note-BBC19Dec-245][^cite_note-BBC13Aug-246] In September 2022, Russia proclaimed the [annexation of four partially-occupied Ukrainian regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annexation_of_southeastern_Ukraine_by_the_Russian_Federation), which was internationally [denounced as illegal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly_Resolution_ES-11/4).[^cite_note-Landay-2022-247] Following the annexations, the conflict has settled into a [war of attrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attrition_warfare) in the [southern](70161012.md) and [eastern fronts](70180401.md), with Russian forces making slow, limited advances and suffering [heavy casualties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casualties_of_the_Russo-Ukrainian_war).[^cite_note-BBC19Dec-245][^cite_note-BBC13Aug-246] Russian forces occupied about a fifth of Ukraine's territory at the end of 2025.[^cite_note-248]&#x20;

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Russia)



[Topographic map](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topographic_map) of Russia
