### Language

Main articles: [Russian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_language) and [Languages of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Russia)

Minority languages across Russia



[Altaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altaic_languages) and [Uralic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uralic_languages) spoken across Russia



The [North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasus) is [ethno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_of_the_Caucasus)-[linguistically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Caucasian_languages) diverse.

[^cite_note-562]

Russian is the [official](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language) and the predominantly spoken language in Russia.[^cite_note-Chevalier-2006-4] It is the most spoken [native language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language) in Europe, the most geographically widespread language of Eurasia, as well as the world's most widely spoken [Slavic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_languages).[^cite_note-language-563] Russian is one of two official languages aboard the [International Space Station](15043.md),[^cite_note-564] as well as one of the six [official languages of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_languages_of_the_United_Nations).[^cite_note-language-563]&#x20;

Russia is a [multilingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multilingualism#Europe) nation: approximately 100–150 minority languages are spoken across the country.[^cite_note-565][^cite_note-566] According to the [Russian Census of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Census_\(2010\)), 137.5 million across the country spoke Russian, 3.1 million spoke [Tatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tatar_language), and 1.1 million spoke [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukrainian_language).[^cite_note-567] The constitution gives the country's individual republics the right to [establish their own state languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_official_languages_in_Russia) in addition to Russian, as well as guarantee its citizens the right to preserve their native language and to create conditions for its study and development.[^cite_note-568] However, various experts have claimed Russia's linguistic diversity is rapidly declining due to [many languages becoming endangered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_endangered_languages_in_Russia).[^cite_note-569][^cite_note-570]&#x20;

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Russia](3060242.md)



[Trinity Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Sunday) in Russia; the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church) has experienced a great revival since the [dissolution of the Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Soviet_Union), a country that had a policy of [state atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_atheism).

Russia is constitutionally a [secular state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_state) that officially enshrines freedom of religion.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998202–220Religion-571][^cite_note-\:2-572] The largest religion is [Eastern Orthodox Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Christianity), chiefly represented by the [Russian Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Orthodox_Church),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998202–220Religion-571][^cite_note-ArenaAtlas2012-573] which is legally recognised for its "special role" in the country's "history and the formation and development of its spirituality and culture."[^cite_note-\:2-572] [Christianity](5211.md), [Islam](6037917.md), [Judaism](15624.md), and [Buddhism](3267529.md) are recognised by Russian law as the "traditional" religions of the country constituting its "historical heritage".[^cite_note-574][^cite_note-575]&#x20;

Islam is the second-largest religion in Russia and is traditional among the majority of [peoples in the North Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_of_the_Caucasus) and some [Turkic peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkic_peoples) in the [Volga-Ural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idel-Ural) region.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998202–220Religion-571][^cite_note-ArenaAtlas2012-573] Large populations of Buddhists are found in [Kalmykia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalmykia), [Buryatia](741441.md), [Zabaykalsky Krai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zabaykalsky_Krai), and they are the vast majority of the population in [Tuva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuva).[^cite_note-ArenaAtlas2012-573] A negligible population practices other religions—such as [Rodnovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodnovery) (Slavic Neopaganism),[^cite_note-576] [Assianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assianism) (Scythian Neopaganism),[^cite_note-577] other ethnic Paganisms, and inter-Pagan movements such as [Ringing Cedars' Anastasianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringing_Cedars%27_Anastasianism),[^cite_note-578] various movements of [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism),[^cite_note-579] [Siberian shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siberian_shamanism)[^cite_note-580] and [Tengrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism), various [Neo-Theosophical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Theosophy) movements such as [Roerichism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roerichism)—among other faiths.[^cite_note-581][^cite_note-582] Some religious minorities have faced oppression and some have been banned in the country:[^cite_note-583] notably, in 2017 the [Jehovah's Witnesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses) were outlawed in Russia, facing persecution ever since, after having been declared an "extremist" and "nontraditional" faith.[^cite_note-584]&#x20;

In 2012, the research organisation Sreda, in cooperation with the [Ministry of Justice](35320735.md), published the Arena Atlas, an adjunct to the 2010 census, enumerating in detail the religious populations and nationalities of Russia, based on a large-sample country-wide survey. The results showed that 47.3% of Russians declared themselves Christians—including 41% Russian Orthodox, 1.5% simply Orthodox or members of non-Russian Orthodox churches, 4.1% unaffiliated Christians, and less than 1% [Old Believers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Believers), [Catholics](606848.md) or [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants)—25% were [believers without affiliation to any specific religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_but_not_religious), 13% were [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism), 6.5% were Muslims,[^cite_note-ArenaAtlasIslam-585] 1.2% were followers of "traditional religions honouring gods and ancestors" ([Rodnovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_Native_Faith), other Paganisms, [Siberian shamanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shamanism_in_Siberia) and [Tengrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tengrism)), 0.5% were Buddhists, 0.1% were [religious Jews](15624.md) and 0.1% were Hindus.[^cite_note-ArenaAtlas2012-573]&#x20;

### Education

Main article: [Education in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Russia)



[Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University), the most prestigious educational institution in Russia

[^cite_note-586]

Russia has a universal adult [literacy rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literate),[^cite_note-587][^cite_note-bite-251] and [education is compulsory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compulsory_education) for the 11 years from age 7 to 17–18.[^cite_note-Nuffic-2019-588] It grants [free education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_education) to its citizens by constitution.[^cite_note-CEPES-589] The [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Russia\)) is responsible for primary and secondary education, as well as vocational education, and there is a [Ministry of Science and Higher Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Science_and_Higher_Education_\(Russia\)).[^cite_note-Nuffic-2019-588] Regional authorities regulate education within their jurisdictions within the prevailing framework of federal laws. As of 2021, over 41% of the Russian population has a [bachelor's degree](188874.md) or an equivalent—which is among the [highest percentages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_tertiary_education_attainment) of [tertiary-level graduates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education) in the world.[^cite_note-590]&#x20;

Russia's [pre-school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-school) education system is highly developed and optional,[^cite_note-591] some four-fifths of children aged 3 to 6 attend day nurseries or kindergartens. Primary school is compulsory for eleven years, starting from age 6 to 7, and leads to a basic general education certificate.[^cite_note-Nuffic-2019-588] An additional two or three years of schooling are required for the secondary-level certificate, and some seven-eighths of Russians continue their education past this level.[^cite_note-Educationb-592]&#x20;

Admission to an institute of higher education is selective and highly competitive:[^cite_note-593] first-degree courses usually take five years.[^cite_note-Educationb-592] The oldest and largest [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_institutions_of_higher_education_in_Russia) in Russia are [Moscow State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_State_University) and [Saint Petersburg State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Petersburg_State_University).[^cite_note-594] There are ten [federal universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Federal_universities_of_Russia) across the country.&#x20;

### Health

Main article: [Health in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Russia)

Further information: [Healthcare in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Russia)

Russia constitutionally guarantees free, [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) for all Russian citizens through the [mandatory medical insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_mandate) (OMS) funded by the [Federal Compulsory Medical Insurance Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Compulsory_Medical_Insurance_Fund_\(Russia\)) (FFOMS) created in 1993.[^cite_note-595][^cite_note-OECDhealth-596][^cite_note-thompson-597] The [Ministry of Health of the Russian Federation](29896200.md) oversees national [healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care). Its healthcare system has decentralized since the 1990s, with federal entities having local healthcare departments, shifting from the centralized and hierarchical Soviet system.[^cite_note-OECDhealth-596] Private healthcare has also seen development, created out of the [public healthcare fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publicly_funded_health_care).[^cite_note-598][^cite_note-thompson-597]&#x20;



Metallurg, a Soviet-era [sanatorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanatorium) in [Sochi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi)

[^cite_note-599]

Russia spent 7.39% of its GDP on healthcare in 2021.[^cite_note-600] Its healthcare expenditure is notably lower than other developed nations.[^cite_note-601] As of 2023, the overall [life expectancy in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_life_expectancy) at birth is 73 years,[^cite_note-602] an increase of roughly 8 years from 2005.[^cite_note-603] Russia has among the highest gender gaps in life expectancy—78 for females and 68 for males—a gap of 10 years.[^cite_note-men-604][^cite_note-605][^cite_note-606] It has one of the most female-biased [sex ratios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_sex_ratio) in the world, with 0.859 males to every female,[^cite_note-cia-258] due to its high male [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate).[^cite_note-men-604][^cite_note-su-607][^cite_note-thompson-597] Russia's [infant mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_mortality) is very low (4 per 1,000 [live births](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live_birth_\(human\))).[^cite_note-608]&#x20;

[Cardiovascular diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardiovascular_disease) account for more than half of the yearly mortalities in Russia,[^cite_note-609] which is one of the highest percentages in the world.[^cite_note-610] Its [alcohol consumption rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcohol_in_Russia) was historically seen as the biggest health issue,[^cite_note-611] but has seen [a stark decrease since 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_alcohol_consumption_per_capita) due to restrictive government measures.[^cite_note-612][^cite_note-613][^cite_note-614] [Obesity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obesity) is a prevalent health issue, with most adults being overweight or obese,[^cite_note-615] alongside [smoking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoking_in_Russia)—Russia's tobacco consumption rate is among the [highest in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevalence_of_tobacco_use).[^cite_note-616] The country's [high suicide rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_suicide_rate) also remains a [significant social issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_in_Russia).[^cite_note-617][^cite_note-su-607]&#x20;

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Russia](2012490.md)

classical building at night

The [Bolshoi Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolshoi_Theatre) in Moscow

Russian culture reflects a long, gradual, and complex amalgamation of various elements that coincided with centuries of development, expansion, and interaction with different peoples, artistic movements, and cultures.[^cite_note-618] Russia has heavily influenced [classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998228–230Music-619][^cite_note-620] [ballet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_ballet),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998230–232Ballet-621][^cite_note-622] theatre,[^cite_note-623] [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_mathematicians),[^cite_note-math-507] [sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Russia),[^cite_note-Riordan-1993-624] [painting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_artists),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625][^cite_note-626] and [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Russia).[^cite_note-Bulgakova-2012-627] [Russian writers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_literature) and [philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_philosophy) have played an important role in the development of [European literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_literature)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-McLean-1962-629] and thought.[^cite_note-630] Russia also made pioneering [contributions to science, technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Russian_inventions_and_technology_records), and [space exploration](28431.md).[^cite_note-631][^cite_note-632]&#x20;

Russia is home to [32](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Russia) UNESCO [World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Heritage_Sites), 21 of which are cultural, while 31 lie on the tentative list.[^cite_note-633] The large global [Russian diaspora](5835042.md) has also played a major role in spreading Russian culture throughout the world. Russia's national symbol, the [double-headed eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double-headed_eagle), dates back to the Tsardom period and is featured in [its coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Russia) and [heraldry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_heraldry).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] The [Russian Bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Bear) and [Mother Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personification_of_Russia) are often used as [national personifications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_personification) of the country.[^cite_note-634][^cite_note-635] [Matryoshka dolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matryoshka_dolls) are a cultural icon of Russia.[^cite_note-636]&#x20;

### Holidays

Main article: [Public holidays in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Russia)

Russia has eight official holidays spanning public, patriotic, and religious commemorations.[^cite_note-637] The year starts with New Year's Day on 1 January, soon followed by [Russian Orthodox Christmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_in_Russia) on 7 January; the two are the country's most popular holidays.[^cite_note-638] [Defender of the Fatherland Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defender_of_the_Fatherland_Day), dedicated to men, is celebrated on 23 February.[^cite_note-639] [International Women's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Women%27s_Day) on 8 March, gained momentum in Russia during the Soviet era. The annual celebration of women has become so popular, especially among Russian men, that the flower vendors of Moscow often see profits "fifteen times" more compared to other holidays.[^cite_note-640] [Spring and Labour Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/May_Day#Russia), originally a Soviet era holiday dedicated to workers, is celebrated on 1 May.[^cite_note-641]&#x20;



The [Scarlet Sails](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarlet_Sails_\(tradition\)) being celebrated along the [Neva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neva) in Saint Petersburg

[Victory Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Day_\(Russia\)), which honours Soviet victory over Nazi Germany and the [End of World War II in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_of_World_War_II_in_Europe), is celebrated on 9 May as an annual [large parade](49466651.md) in Moscow's Red Square[^cite_note-642] and marks the famous [Immortal Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immortal_Regiment) civil event.[^cite_note-643] Other patriotic holidays include [Russia Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia_Day) on 12 June, celebrated to commemorate Russia's [declaration of sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_State_Sovereignty_of_the_Russian_Soviet_Federative_Socialist_Republic) from the collapsing Soviet Union,[^cite_note-644] and [Unity Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unity_Day_\(Russia\)) on 4 November, commemorating the [1612 uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Moscow_\(1612\)) that marked the end of the [Polish occupation of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish%E2%80%93Russian_War_\(1609%E2%80%931618\)).[^cite_note-645]&#x20;

There are many popular non-public holidays. [Old New Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_New_Year) is celebrated on 14 January.[^cite_note-646] [Maslenitsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maslenitsa) is an ancient and popular East Slavic folk holiday.[^cite_note-647] [Cosmonautics Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmonautics_Day) on 12 April, in tribute to the first human trip into space.[^cite_note-648] Two major Christian holidays are [Easter](9325.md) and [Trinity Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity_Sunday).[^cite_note-649]&#x20;

### Art and architecture

Main articles: [Russian artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_artists), [Architecture of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Russia), and [List of Russian architects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Russian_architects)

Early Russian painting is [represented in icons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_icons) and vibrant [frescos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco). In the early 15th century, master icon painter [Andrei Rublev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Rublev) created some of Russia's most treasured religious art.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625] The [Russian Academy of Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Academy_of_Arts), which was established in 1757 to train Russian artists, brought Western techniques of secular painting to Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998Chapter_1–2._Historical_Setting-59] In the 18th century, academicians [Ivan Argunov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Argunov), [Dmitry Levitzky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Levitzky), [Vladimir Borovikovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Borovikovsky) became influential.[^cite_note-650] The early 19th century saw many prominent paintings by [Karl Briullov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Briullov) and [Alexander Ivanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Andreyevich_Ivanov), both of whom were known for [Romantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanticism) historical canvases.[^cite_note-651][^cite_note-652] [Ivan Aivazovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Aivazovsky), another Romantic painter, is considered one of the greatest masters of [marine art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_art).[^cite_note-653]&#x20;



[Karl Bryullov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Bryullov), *[The Last Day of Pompeii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Day_of_Pompeii)* (1833)



The [Winter Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_Palace) served as the [official residence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_residence) of the [Emperor of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_of_all_the_Russias).

In the 1860s, a group of critical [realists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realism_\(arts\)) ([Peredvizhniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peredvizhniki)), led by [Ivan Kramskoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Kramskoy), [Ilya Repin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilya_Repin) and [Vasiliy Perov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasiliy_Perov) broke with the academy, and portrayed the many-sided aspects of social life in paintings.[^cite_note-654][^cite_note-655] The turn of the 20th century saw the rise of [symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbolism_\(arts\)), represented by [Mikhail Vrubel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Vrubel) and [Nicholas Roerich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Roerich).[^cite_note-656][^cite_note-657] The [Russian avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_avant-garde) flourished from approximately 1890 to 1930; globally influential artists from this era were [El Lissitzky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Lissitzky),[^cite_note-658] [Kazimir Malevich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kazimir_Malevich), [Natalia Goncharova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natalia_Goncharova), [Wassily Kandinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wassily_Kandinsky), and [Marc Chagall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marc_Chagall).[^cite_note-659]&#x20;

The history of [Russian architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Russia) begins with early woodcraft buildings of ancient Slavs and the [church architecture of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture_of_Kievan_Rus%27).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625][^cite_note-660] The [Christianization of Kievan Rus'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization_of_Kievan_Rus%27) brought centuries [Byzantine architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_architecture#Legacy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625][^cite_note-661] Following Mongol occupation, Kievan Rus' cut its ties with the Byzantine Empire, and Russian architecture saw native innovations, such as the invention of the [iconostasis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconostasis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625] [Aristotle Fioravanti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_Fioravanti) and other Italian architects brought [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance) trends to the [Grand Principality of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Principality_of_Moscow), which influenced the reconstruction of the [Moscow Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625][^cite_note-662] The 16th century saw the development of the unique [tent-like churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tent-like_church) and the [onion dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onion_dome) design, which is a distinctive feature of Russian architecture.[^cite_note-663] In the 17th century, the "fiery style" of ornamentation flourished in Moscow and [Yaroslavl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaroslavl), gradually paving the way for the [Naryshkin baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naryshkin_baroque) of the 1680s.[^cite_note-664]&#x20;

After the reforms of Peter the Great, Russia's architecture became influenced by Western European styles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625] The 18th-century taste for [Rococo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rococo) architecture led to the [works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elizabethan_Baroque) of [Bartolomeo Rastrelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartolomeo_Rastrelli) and his followers. The most influential Russian architects of the eighteenth century, [Vasily Bazhenov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Bazhenov), [Matvey Kazakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matvey_Kazakov), and [Ivan Starov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Starov), created lasting monuments in Moscow and Saint Petersburg and established a base for the more Russian forms that followed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625] During the reign of Catherine the Great, Saint Petersburg was transformed into an outdoor museum of [Neoclassical architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture).[^cite_note-665] Under Alexander I, [Empire style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_style) became the *de facto* architectural style.[^cite_note-666] The second half of the 19th century was dominated by the [Neo-Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Byzantine_architecture_in_the_Russian_Empire) and [Russian Revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Revival) style.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998232–233Architecture_and_Painting-625][^cite_note-667] In the early 20th century, [Russian neoclassical revival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_neoclassical_revival) became a trend.[^cite_note-668] Prevalent styles of the late 20th century were [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau_architecture_in_Russia),[^cite_note-669] [Constructivism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constructivism_\(art\)),[^cite_note-670] and [Socialist Classicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalinist_architecture).[^cite_note-671]&#x20;

### Music

Main article: [Music of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Russia)



[Pyotr Ilyich Tchaikovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyotr_Ilyich_Tchaikovsky) (1840–1893), c. 1888

Until the 18th century, music in Russia consisted mainly of church music and folk songs and dances.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998228–230Music-619] In the 19th century, it was defined by the tension between classical composer [Mikhail Glinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Glinka) along with other members of [The Mighty Handful](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mighty_Handful), who were later succeeded by the [Belyayev circle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belyayev_circle),[^cite_note-672] and the [Russian Musical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Musical_Society) led by composers [Anton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Rubinstein) and [Nikolay Rubinstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Rubinstein).[^cite_note-673] The later tradition of [Pyotr Ilyich Tchaikovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyotr_Ilyich_Tchaikovsky), one of the greatest composers of the Romantic era, was continued into the 20th century by [Sergei Rachmaninoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Rachmaninoff). World-renowned composers of the 20th century include [Alexander Scriabin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Scriabin), [Alexander Glazunov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Glazunov),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998228–230Music-619] [Igor Stravinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Stravinsky), [Sergei Prokofiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Prokofiev) and [Dmitri Shostakovich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitri_Shostakovich), and later [Edison Denisov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edison_Denisov), [Sofia Gubaidulina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sofia_Gubaidulina),[^cite_note-music2-674] [Georgy Sviridov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgy_Sviridov),[^cite_note-675] and [Alfred Schnittke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Schnittke).[^cite_note-music2-674]&#x20;

During the Soviet era, [popular music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_music) also produced a number of renowned figures, such as the two [balladeers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bard_\(Soviet_Union\))—[Vladimir Vysotsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Vysotsky) and [Bulat Okudzhava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulat_Okudzhava),[^cite_note-music2-674] and performers such as [Alla Pugacheva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alla_Pugacheva).[^cite_note-676] [Jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), even with sanctions from Soviet authorities, flourished and evolved into one of the country's most popular musical forms.[^cite_note-music2-674] By the 1980s, [rock music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_music_in_Russia) became popular across Russia, and produced bands such as [Aria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aria_\(band\)), [Aquarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquarium_\(band\)),[^cite_note-677] [DDT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DDT_\(band\)),[^cite_note-678] and [Kino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kino_\(band\));[^cite_note-679] the latter's leader [Viktor Tsoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Tsoi), was in particular, a gigantic figure.[^cite_note-680] [Pop music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_pop) has continued to flourish in Russia since the 1960s, with globally famous acts such as [t.A.T.u.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T.A.T.u.)[^cite_note-681]&#x20;

### Literature and philosophy

Main articles: [Russian literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_literature) and [Russian philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_philosophy)



First page of the [Novgorod Codex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novgorod_Codex), c. 1000, the oldest surviving book of Kievan Rus'

Russian literature is among the world's most influential and developed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-McLean-1962-629] It can be traced to the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages), when [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic) was introduced as a [liturgical language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_language) and came to be used as a [literary language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_language), creating a situation of [diglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222Literature-682][^cite_note-Cornwell-683]<sup>: 4 </sup> The Russian vernacular remained in use for [oral literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_literature) and chancery writing; it gradually supplanted [Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Slavonic) in secular works, contributing to the standardisation of the modern Russian literary language in the 18th and early 19th centuries.[^cite_note-Cornwell-683]<sup>: 5 </sup>&#x20;

By the [Age of Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment), literature had grown in importance, with works from [Mikhail Lomonosov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Lomonosov), [Denis Fonvizin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denis_Fonvizin), [Gavrila Derzhavin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gavrila_Derzhavin), and [Nikolay Karamzin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Karamzin).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] From the early 1830s, during the [Golden Age of Russian Poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Age_of_Russian_Poetry), literature underwent an astounding golden age in poetry, prose and drama.[^cite_note-684] [Romantic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_literature) permitted a flowering of poetic talent: [Vasily Zhukovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Zhukovsky) and later his protégé [Alexander Pushkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Pushkin) came to the fore.[^cite_note-685] Following Pushkin's footsteps, a new generation of poets were born, including [Mikhail Lermontov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Lermontov), [Nikolay Nekrasov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Nekrasov), [Aleksey Konstantinovich Tolstoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksey_Konstantinovich_Tolstoy), [Fyodor Tyutchev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Tyutchev), and [Afanasy Fet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afanasy_Fet).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628]&#x20;

The first great Russian novelist was [Nikolai Gogol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Gogol).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-686] Then, during the [Age of Realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literary_realism),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] came [Ivan Turgenev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Turgenev), who mastered both short stories and novels.[^cite_note-687] [Fyodor Dostoevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoyevsky) and [Leo Tolstoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Tolstoy) soon became internationally renowned.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] [Mikhail Saltykov-Shchedrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Saltykov-Shchedrin) wrote prose [satire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-688] while [Nikolai Leskov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Leskov) is best remembered for his shorter fiction.[^cite_note-689] In the second half of the century [Anton Chekhov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anton_Chekhov) excelled in short stories and became a leading dramatist.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-690] Other important 19th-century developments included the fabulist [Ivan Krylov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Krylov),[^cite_note-691] non-fiction writers such as the critic [Vissarion Belinsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vissarion_Belinsky),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628][^cite_note-692] and playwrights such as [Aleksandr Griboyedov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Griboyedov) and [Aleksandr Ostrovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Ostrovsky).[^cite_note-693][^cite_note-694] The beginning of the 20th century ranks as the [Silver Age of Russian Poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_Age_of_Russian_Poetry).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] This era had poets such as [Alexander Blok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Blok), [Anna Akhmatova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Akhmatova), [Boris Pasternak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Pasternak), [Vladimir Mayakovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Mayakovsky),[^cite_note-695] [Sergei Yesenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Yesenin),[^cite_note-696] and [Konstantin Balmont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konstantin_Balmont).[^cite_note-697] It also produced some first-rate novelists and short-story writers, such as [Aleksandr Kuprin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Kuprin), Nobel Prize winner [Ivan Bunin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivan_Bunin), [Leonid Andreyev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Andreyev), [Yevgeny Zamyatin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yevgeny_Zamyatin), [Dmitry Merezhkovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dmitry_Merezhkovsky), and [Andrei Bely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Bely).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628]&#x20;

After the Russian Revolution of 1917, Russian literature split into Soviet and [white émigré](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_%C3%A9migr%C3%A9) parts. In the 1930s, [socialist realism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_realism) became the predominant trend in Russia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] Its leading figure was [Maxim Gorky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maxim_Gorky), who laid the foundations of this style.[^cite_note-698] [Mikhail Bulgakov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bulgakov) was one of the leading writers of the Soviet era.[^cite_note-699] [Nikolay Ostrovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Ostrovsky)'s novel *[How the Steel Was Tempered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/How_the_Steel_Was_Tempered)* has been among the most successful works of Russian literature.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998222–228Literature-628] Influential émigré writers include [Vladimir Nabokov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Nabokov),[^cite_note-700] [Joseph Brodsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Brodsky),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998227Literature-701] and [Isaac Asimov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Asimov), who was considered one of the "Big Three" science fiction writers.[^cite_note-702] Some writers dared to oppose Soviet ideology, such as Nobel Prize-winning novelist [Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandr_Solzhenitsyn), who wrote about life in the Gulag camps,[^cite_note-703] and [Andrey Sinyavsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrey_Sinyavsky).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998227Literature-701]&#x20;



[Leo Tolstoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leo_Tolstoy) (1828–1910) is widely regarded as one of the greatest authors of all time, with works such as *[War and Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_and_Peace)*.[^cite_note-704]



[Fyodor Dostoevsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fyodor_Dostoevsky) (1821–1881), widely considered one of the greatest novelists of all time, whose works include *[Crime and Punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_and_Punishment)*[^cite_note-705]

Russian literature faced rapid and difficult changes during the turbulent 1990s, with writers and publishers struggling to adjust to new economic and political developments.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998227–228Literature-706][^cite_note-Cornwell-683]<sup>: 65 </sup> Domestic literature subsequently declined in influence among most Russians,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurtis1998228Literature-707] who now had sudden and rapid access to a wide volume of previously suppressed Western literary movements.[^cite_note-auto-708] Nevertheless, this environment fostered experimental and postmodern literature and satire.[^cite_note-auto-708] At the beginning of the 21st century, the most discussed figures, [postmodernists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_postmodernism) [Victor Pelevin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Pelevin) and [Vladimir Sorokin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Sorokin), remained the leading Russian writers.[^cite_note-709][^cite_note-Cornwell-683]<sup>: 65–67 </sup>&#x20;

Russian philosophy has been influential. Religious and spiritual philosophy is represented by [Vladimir Solovyov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Solovyov_\(philosopher\)), [Nikolai Berdyaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Berdyaev), [Pavel Florensky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pavel_Florensky), [Semyon Frank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semyon_Frank), [Nikolay Lossky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolay_Lossky), [Vasily Rozanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasily_Rozanov), and others.[^cite_note-710] Mystic [Helena Blavatsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helena_Blavatsky) gained an international following as the leading theoretician of [Theosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosophy) and the co-founder of the [Theosophical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theosophical_Society).[^cite_note-711] [Alexander Herzen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Herzen) is known as one of the fathers of [agrarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrarianism) [populism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism).[^cite_note-712] [Mikhail Bakunin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bakunin) is referred to as the father of [anarchism](12.md).[^cite_note-713] [Peter Kropotkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Kropotkin) was the most important theorist of [anarcho-communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarcho-communism).[^cite_note-714] [Mikhail Bakhtin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Bakhtin)'s writings have significantly inspired scholars in various fields.[^cite_note-715] [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin), a major revolutionary, developed a variant of communism known as [Leninism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leninism).[^cite_note-716] [Leon Trotsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leon_Trotsky), Lenin's contemporary and co-revolutionary, founded his own strain of Marxism known as [Trotskyism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trotskyism).[^cite_note-717] [Alexander Zinoviev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Zinoviev) was a prominent philosopher and writer in the second half of the 20th century.[^cite_note-718]&#x20;

### Mass media and cinema

Main articles: [Media of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Russia) and [Cinema of Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinema_of_Russia)



[Ostankino Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostankino_Tower) in Moscow, the [tallest freestanding structure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_freestanding_structures) in Europe

[^cite_note-719]

There are 400 news agencies in Russia, among which the largest internationally operating are [TASS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TASS), [RIA Novosti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RIA_Novosti), [Sputnik](44384287.md), and [Interfax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interfax).[^cite_note-720] [Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Russia) is the most popular medium in Russia.[^cite_note-bbcmedia-721] Among the 3,000 licensed radio stations nationwide, notable ones include [Radio Rossii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Rossii), [Vesti FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesti_FM), [Echo of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo_of_Moscow), [Radio Mayak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Mayak), and [Russkoye Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russkoye_Radio). Of the 16,000 registered newspapers, *[Argumenty i Fakty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argumenty_i_Fakty)*, *[Komsomolskaya Pravda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Komsomolskaya_Pravda)*, *[Rossiyskaya Gazeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rossiyskaya_Gazeta)*, *[Izvestia](682324.md)*, and *[Moskovskij Komsomolets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moskovskij_Komsomolets)* are popular. State-run [Channel One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_One_Russia) and [Russia-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia-1) are the leading news channels, while [RT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RT_\(TV_network\)) is the flagship of Russia's international media operations.[^cite_note-bbcmedia-721]&#x20;

Russian and later [Soviet cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_cinema) was a hotbed of invention, resulting in world-renowned films such as *[Battleship Potemkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battleship_Potemkin)*, which was named the [greatest film of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_considered_the_best) at the [Brussels World's Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expo_58) in 1958.[^cite_note-722][^cite_note-723] Soviet-era filmmakers, most notably [Sergei Eisenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergei_Eisenstein) and [Andrei Tarkovsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_Tarkovsky), would go on to become among of the world's most innovative and influential directors.[^cite_note-724][^cite_note-725] Eisenstein was a student of [Lev Kuleshov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lev_Kuleshov), who developed the groundbreaking [Soviet montage theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_montage_theory) of film editing at the world's first film school, the [All-Union Institute of Cinematography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerasimov_Institute_of_Cinematography).[^cite_note-726] [Dziga Vertov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dziga_Vertov)'s "[Kino-Eye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kino-Eye)" theory had a large effect on the development of documentary filmmaking and cinema realism.[^cite_note-727] Many Soviet socialist realism films were artistically successful, including *[Chapaev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chapaev_\(film\))*, *[The Cranes Are Flying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Cranes_Are_Flying)*, and *[Ballad of a Soldier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballad_of_a_Soldier)*.[^cite_note-Bulgakova-2012-627]&#x20;

The 1960s and 1970s saw a greater variety of artistic styles in Soviet cinema.[^cite_note-Bulgakova-2012-627] The comedies of [Eldar Ryazanov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eldar_Ryazanov) and [Leonid Gaidai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Gaidai) were immensely popular, with many of their catchphrases still in use today.[^cite_note-728][^cite_note-729] In 1961–68 [Sergey Bondarchuk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergey_Bondarchuk) directed an [Oscar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award)-winning [film adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_and_Peace_\(film_series\)) of Leo Tolstoy's epic *[War and Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_and_Peace)*, which was [the most expensive film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_most_expensive_film) made in the Soviet Union.[^cite_note-Bulgakova-2012-627] In 1969, [Vladimir Motyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Motyl)'s *[White Sun of the Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sun_of_the_Desert)* was released, a very popular film in a genre of [ostern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostern); the film is traditionally watched by [cosmonauts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmonauts) before any trip into space.[^cite_note-730] After the dissolution of the Soviet Union, the Russian cinema industry suffered large losses; however, since the late 2000s, it has seen growth once again, and continues to expand.[^cite_note-731]&#x20;

### Cuisine

Main article: [Russian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_cuisine)



[Kvass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kvass) is an ancient and traditional Russian beverage.

Russian cuisine has been formed by the country's diverse climate, cultural and religious traditions, and vast geography; it shares similarities with neighbouring countries. Crops of [rye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rye), wheat, [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley), and [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet) provide the ingredients for various breads, [pancakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pancake) and cereals, as well as for many drinks. [Bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bread_in_Europe#Finland_and_Russia), of many varieties,[^cite_note-732] is very popular across Russia.[^cite_note-733] Flavourful soups and stews include [shchi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shchi), [borsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Borsch), [ukha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukha), [solyanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solyanka), and [okroshka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okroshka). [Smetana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smetana_\(dairy_product\)) (a heavy [sour cream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sour_cream)) and [mayonnaise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayonnaise) are often added to soups and salads.[^cite_note-734][^cite_note-735] [Pirozhki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirozhki),[^cite_note-736] [blini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blini),[^cite_note-737] and [syrniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrniki) are native types of pancakes.[^cite_note-738] [Beef Stroganoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beef_Stroganoff),[^cite_note-Volokh-1983-739]<sup>: 266 </sup> [Chicken Kiev](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicken_Kiev),[^cite_note-Volokh-1983-739]<sup>: 320 </sup> [pelmeni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelmeni),[^cite_note-740] and [shashlyk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shashlyk) are popular meat dishes.[^cite_note-741] Other meat dishes include stuffed cabbage rolls ([golubtsy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golubtsy)) usually filled with meat.[^cite_note-742] Salads include [Olivier salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olivier_salad),[^cite_note-743] [vinegret](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vinegret),[^cite_note-744] and [dressed herring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dressed_herring).[^cite_note-745]&#x20;

Russia's [national non-alcoholic drink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_drinks) is [kvass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kvass),[^cite_note-746] and the national alcoholic drink is [vodka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vodka); its production in Russia (and elsewhere) dates back to the 14th century.[^cite_note-747] The country has the world's highest vodka consumption,[^cite_note-748] while [beer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Russia) is the most popular alcoholic beverage.[^cite_note-749] [Wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_wine) has become increasingly popular in Russia in the 21st century.[^cite_note-750] [Tea has been popular in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_tea_culture) for centuries.[^cite_note-751]&#x20;

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Russia)



[KHL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kontinental_Hockey_League) match between [CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSKA_Moscow) and [Dynamo Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HC_Dynamo_Moscow) at [CSKA Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSKA_Arena), [Moscow](19004.md)

[Football](10568.md) is the most popular sport in Russia.[^cite_note-752] The [Soviet Union national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_national_football_team) became the first European champions by winning [Euro 1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_1960),[^cite_note-753] and reached the finals of [Euro 1988](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_1988).[^cite_note-754] Russian clubs [CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PFC_CSKA_Moscow) and [Zenit Saint Petersburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenit_Saint_Petersburg) won the [UEFA Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Cup) in 2005 and 2008.[^cite_note-755][^cite_note-756] The [Russian national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_national_football_team) reached the semi-finals of [Euro 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_2008).[^cite_note-757] Russia was the host nation for the [2017 FIFA Confederations Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_FIFA_Confederations_Cup),[^cite_note-758] and the [2018 FIFA World Cup](13327177.md).[^cite_note-759] However, Russian teams are currently suspended from FIFA and UEFA competitions.[^cite_note-760]&#x20;

[Ice hockey](19428477.md) is very popular in Russia, and the [Soviet national ice hockey team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union_men%27s_national_ice_hockey_team) dominated the sport internationally throughout its existence.[^cite_note-Riordan-1993-624] [Bandy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandy) is Russia's national sport, and it has historically been the highest-achieving country in the sport.[^cite_note-761] The [Russian national basketball team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_national_basketball_team) won [EuroBasket 2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroBasket_2007),[^cite_note-762] and the Russian basketball club [PBC CSKA Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PBC_CSKA_Moscow) is among the most successful European basketball teams.[^cite_note-763] The annual Formula One [Russian Grand Prix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Grand_Prix) was held at the [Sochi Autodrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi_Autodrom) in the [Sochi Olympic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sochi_Olympic_Park), until its termination following the [2022 Russian invasion of Ukraine](79400268.md).[^cite_note-764][^cite_note-765]&#x20;

Historically, [Russian athletes](3787642.md) have been one of the most successful contenders in the [Olympic Games](22576.md).[^cite_note-Riordan-1993-624] Russia is the leading nation in [rhythmic gymnastics](189053.md), and Russian [synchronised swimming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronised_swimming) is considered to be the world's best.[^cite_note-766] [Figure skating](11152.md) is another popular sport in Russia, especially [pair skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pair_skating) and [ice dancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_dancing).[^cite_note-767] Russia has produced numerous prominent tennis players.[^cite_note-768] [Chess](5134.md) is also a widely popular pastime in the nation, with many of the world's top chess players being Russian for decades.[^cite_note-769] The [1980 Summer Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_Summer_Olympic_Games) were held in Moscow,[^cite_note-770] and the [2014 Winter Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Winter_Olympics) and the [2014 Winter Paralympics](2311504.md) were hosted in Sochi.[^cite_note-771][^cite_note-772] However, Russia has also had 43 [Olympic medals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_medal) stripped from its athletes due to [doping violations](55980489.md), which is the most of any country, and nearly a third of the global total.[^cite_note-773]&#x20;

[^cite_note-18]: &#x20;The fourteen countries bordering Russia are [Norway](21241.md) and [Finland](10577.md) to the northwest; [Estonia](28222445.md), [Latvia](17514.md), [Belarus](3457.md) and [Ukraine](31750.md) to the west, as well as [Lithuania](17675.md) and [Poland](22936.md) (with [Kaliningrad Oblast](17311.md)); [Georgia](48768.md) and [Azerbaijan](746.md) to the southwest; [Kazakhstan](16642.md) and [Mongolia](19271.md) to the south; and [China](5405.md) and [North Korea](21255.md) to the southeast.<sup>[\[13\]](#cite_note-17)</sup> Russia also shares [maritime boundaries](29613061.md) with Japan and the United States, as well as borders with the two [partially recognised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partially_recognized_states) breakaway states of [South Ossetia](318381.md) and [Abkhazia](18933375.md) that it occupies in Georgia.

[^cite_note-211]: &#x20;Most notably the [Budyonnovsk hospital hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budyonnovsk_hospital_hostage_crisis), the [Russian apartment bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_apartment_bombings), the [Moscow theatre hostage crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_theater_hostage_crisis), and the [Beslan school siege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beslan_school_siege)

[^cite_note-253]: &#x20;Russia has an additional 850 km (530 mi) of coastline along the [Caspian Sea](19653787.md), which is the world's largest inland body of water, and has been variously classified as a sea or a lake.<sup>[\[246\]](#cite_note-252)</sup>

[^cite_note-256]: &#x20;Russia, by land area, is larger than the continents of [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia_\(continent\)), [Antarctica](18959138.md), and Europe, although it covers a large part of the latter itself. Its land area could be roughly compared to that of South America.

[^cite_note-262]: &#x20;Russia borders, clockwise, to its southwest: the [Black Sea](3386.md) and the [Sea of Azov](78019.md), to its west: the [Baltic Sea](3335.md), to its north: the [Barents Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barents_Sea) ([White Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Sea), [Pechora Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechora_Sea)), the [Kara Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kara_Sea), the [Laptev Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laptev_Sea), and the [East Siberian Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Siberian_Sea), to its northeast: the [Chukchi Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chukchi_Sea) and the [Bering Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bering_Sea), and to its southeast: the [Sea of Okhotsk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Okhotsk) and the [Sea of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_of_Japan).

[^cite_note-289]: &#x20;In 2020, [constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Russia) were signed into law that limit the president to two terms overall rather than two consecutive terms, with this limit reset for current and previous presidents.<sup>[\[279\]](#cite_note-288)</sup>

[^cite_note-disputed-315]: &#x20;Including bodies on territory disputed between Russia and Ukraine whose annexation has not been internationally recognised: the [Republic of Crimea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Crimea_\(Russia\)) and the federal city of [Sevastopol](51587.md) since the annexation of Crimea in 2014,<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-Pifer-2020-1)</sup> and territories set up following the [Russian annexation of Donetsk, Kherson, Luhansk and Zaporizhzhia oblasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_annexation_of_Donetsk,_Kherson,_Luhansk_and_Zaporizhzhia_oblasts) in 2022.

[^cite_note-ArenaAtlasIslam-585]: &#x20;The Sreda Arena Atlas 2012 did not count the populations of two federal subjects of Russia where the majority of the population is Muslim, namely [Chechnya](6095.md) and [Ingushetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ingushetia), which together had a population of nearly 2 million, thus the proportion of Muslims was possibly slightly underestimated.<sup>[\[562\]](#cite_note-ArenaAtlas2012-573)</sup>
